#7011. The 5 Types of Coats Every Guy Should Own

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A great winter outfit starts with the right outerwear.

Years ago, I was talking to a very menswear-y buddy at a very menswear-y event, and he proposed a theory of outerwear that’s stuck with me: Your coat is your car. Sounds pretty … menswear-y, right? But bear with me here, because I think it actually makes perfect sense, so long as you’re willing to make a couple (major) concessions.

No, your coat cannot help you travel at 75 miles per hour down an interstate. You cannot fill it up with gasoline or top it up at a charging station. It contains, ideally, zero percent steel.

But if you live in a place where you spend more time walking or taking public transit than you do behind the wheel, your coat does indeed become quite car-like. You ensconce yourself in it when you move about the world. It’s the first thing people see when you arrive and the last thing they see when you depart. It shields you from the elements while simultaneously signaling to friends and strangers alike your combined senses of utility and aesthetics. So, yeah, if you squint and look at the situation sideways, your coat is your car. Even if you live somewhere that demands personal automotive transportation, your coat is your second car—unless you’re one of those kooky types who just dart from the interior of a sedan to the interior of a restaurant in nothing but a sweater—for all the reasons stated above.

The point is: Your coat is the most visible and functional part of your winter wardrobe, and you should approach the buying and wearing of said coat with all due respect for its position. Which is exactly why I created this guide. We’ll run through the key styles that every guy should know, where to get a great example (or three!) of each, and how to wear them with style all season long. You won’t find truckers or bombers here—those are jackets—but you will find parkas, topcoats, trenches, and more. All of them will serve you well until the spring thaw, so let’s get into it.

If you’ve been keeping up with Esquire style guides, you’ll know that we’ve covered a lot of ground over the past few years, breaking down everything from the shoe styles you should have in your rotation to the winter essentials you shouldn’t go without. One story that really resonated with readers was about sweaters. In it, we talked about the fact that you can score very good knitwear from a huge range of lifestyle brands these days. We also mentioned the prudence of keeping a few category specialists on your radar. The same advice holds true for coats.

On the lifestyle side, the brands will look familiar to longtime readers. You’ve got Todd Synder and Buck Mason. J. Press and J. Crew. Ralph Lauren and Drake’s and Alpha Industries and Madewell and Billy Reid and Filson ... the list goes on. You’ve also got fashion behemoths like Burberry, which famously started out with the trench coat. And then you’ve got brands that may not just do outerwear but are truly steeped in it. That could be a heritage player like Mackintosh or Schott or Gloverall, a parka expert like Canada Goose, or an upstart label like Wythe, which makes a wonderful range of products but has a cult following for its exceptional overcoats.

As we’ve warned before: This list is not exhaustive. But it’s a good place to start. Now, on to the coats themselves.

This is the dressiest of the bunch, though it wasn’t always. If you’re a real traditionalist, you might have a velvet-collared Chesterfield in your closet to wear with your fanciest togs. Most of us, though, don’t really need something that formal. The topcoat is similar to and derives from the Chesterfield, but it’s less stuffy.

Still, it looks plenty dressed up to modern eyes. It’ll have lapels, which can be notch or peak (for a little extra personality), and can be single- or double-breasted. If you suit up with any regularity, that means it’s likely the most essential for your wardrobe. Look for one made of wool or a wool blend; this is not the time to overspend on synthetics.

Colors are usually pretty conservative—think camel, navy, gray, and black. That might seem limiting, but remember that this thing should be able to play nicely with your whole wardrobe, so opting for a more subdued hue is usually the right move. As for length: Some folks will swear something that hits above the knee looks more modern. We just think it looks off. Go for at or below the knee.

(Oh, and if you’re wondering if a classic polo coat really counts as a topcoat as opposed to an overcoat, well, you might be right to question us. Technically. But considering its cultural associations—rich New Englanders, Wall Street power brokers—we’re giving it a home here.)

Eschewed by many as a bit too fusty and old-fashioned as recently as a decade ago, the raglan overcoat has come roaring back into the spotlight in recent years. We enthusiastically endorse its return.

The style is defined by its raglan shoulder construction, in which a diagonal seam runs from around the collarbone to the underarm, allowing for an uninterrupted panel over the top of the shoulder. Don’t worry too much about the technical details, though. The main thing to remember is that this shape is more relaxed and tends to layer over other garments (a chunky sweater, a sport coat) more easily and comfortably than a traditional “set in” shoulder seam.

The raglan overcoat also tends to be cut wide and long, so you can bundle up underneath it and never worry about chilly knees. It leans more casual than a topcoat, for sure, but you can still wear it with a suit. And it lends polish to something as simple as jeans and a sweatshirt. One of our favorite subgenres of raglan overcoat is the balmacaan, which features a Prussian collar (which looks like a shirt collar instead of a lapel), a “fly front” with a placket that covers the buttons, and buttoned fasteners at the wrists so you can cinch up to keep out the cold.

Wythe Raglan Overcoat in Diamond Ombre Tweed

If you’re into the warm and wooly vibes of the two styles above but simply can’t stand a long coat, the peacoat is for you. Double-breasted and hip length, it’s a shorter—but no less capable—take on the woolen overcoat that dates back about three centuries.

Originally created for sailors of European (and, eventually, American) navies, the peacoat is tough enough to defend from open-ocean winds and sea spray, which means it’s definitely up to the task of keeping you toasty on your way to work. There are a lot of versions out there these days that are done up in frankly wimpy wool blends that might feel nice at first but don’t do the job.

Go for a heavyweight wool like melton, but make sure there’s enough room to layer up underneath it—and keep an eye on the lapels. They should be big. Big enough that you can pop that collar and shield yourself from an incoming gust without feeling even a tickle of wind touch your neck. Plus, it looks cool.

J. Crew Dock Peacoat in Wool Blend with PrimaLoft

Thanks to a quirky little phenomenon we like to call “global climate change,” there’s a very good chance that some of the winter days ahead will feel a lot more like fall or spring. On those days—especially if it’s raining—you should reach for a trench coat or a mac. The two styles aren’t synonymous, but they’re related in both vibe and history, so we’re coupling them here.

Maximalists will prefer the trench, with its storm flap and epaulettes and double-breasted closure. Minimalists will reach for the mac, with its clean lines and streamlined shape. It’s really a matter of preference, because both fulfill the same roles in your outerwear lineup.

They’ll keep you warmish, especially when combined with additional layers. They’ll help block the wind when it kicks up. And although we can’t guarantee you’ll walk away dry if you stand around for an hour during a deluge, they’ll fend off a shower—or a sprint through a thunderstorm—quite capably.

Burberry Long Kensington Heritage Trench Coat

If it’s really fucking frigid, you need a parka. Make sure it’s properly insulated, whether with a synthetic fill or down. And unless you like freezing your literal ass off, get one that’s hip length or longer. You could get something technical and bright that looks like it just stepped off the ski slopes, but we’re more inclined toward rugged-looking styles that take their cues from the military and outdoor exploration.

The N-3B, or “snorkel parka,” is a burly beast that looks absolutely fantastic with a casual outfit and, if you ask us, can be fun with a suit too. (Though if you’re worried about raising eyebrows or breaking a dress code, maybe opt for a topcoat instead.) Something like Canada Goose’s Langdon parka is a little more pared back in looks but overdelivers on warmth, meaning you’ll be cozy in even the worst weather. The trick is finding one that balances form and function so that it looks as though you intended to wear it on the way to the bar—not like you wandered off some snowcapped mountain and forgot to change.

Like the peacoat, the duffle coat is a part of maritime history. It was adopted by the British Royal Navy in the late 19th century and widely issued to British troops during World War II. Thanks to the sheer number produced and postwar surplus, it quickly became a civilian staple and eventually hopped across the pond to become a fixture of the American preppy uniform. Made of thick wool and equipped with a big hood, it’s very warm. And thanks to the large toggle closures, you don’t even need to take your gloves off to fasten it. Is it a little odd? Perhaps. But that’s what makes it so charming.

#7007. Your Horoscope for the Full Moon in Gemini

Everything is about to hit just a bit harder this go around.

Full Moons are a time for releasing and relinquishing. If you have an excess in your life—emotional clutter, overcommitments, toxic attachments—this is a cosmic cue to drain it out. Whether through silent meditation, honest conversations, or grounding spiritual rituals, Full Moons offer us a ritual checkpoint to let go.

November’s Full Moon in Gemini marks a key turning point, astrologically hinting that it’s time to release the stories we’ve been telling ourselves. Following Western astrology, these New and Full Moon guides are monthly reminders to rest, reflect, and recommit to your path. Your horoscope is less of a prediction and more of a sacred prompt for self-inquiry.

On November 24, 2026, at 9:53 a.m. ET, the Moon becomes full in Gemini, directly opposing the Sun in Sagittarius. Known as the Beaver Moon, the name traditionally associated with the season when beavers prepare for winter and trappers once set their traps before the swamps froze.

This Full Moon is also a Supermoon, so everything about it lands a little louder and closer than usual. Symbolically, it reminds us that preparing for a new season is not only about what we gather, but about what we are finally honest about. As nature continues its transition toward winter, this Full Moon encourages us to examine the stories we have been carrying. Some of them are true. Some of them were only ever our best guess.

The Beaver Moon in Gemini invites us to look at what we thought we knew—about our values, about our feelings, about the people in our lives—and ask whether the story still holds. This lunation reminds us that clarity often begins with admitting we had it backwards.

This Full Moon centers on truth, clarity, and the stories we tell ourselves. With the Moon at 2 degrees of Gemini, we are really starting to have to rethink what we thought we knew about our values and how we were expressing our emotions. Please understand that very often, people aren't exactly being fully honest with themselves—and it's not because they're consciously lying. Sometimes they don't want to fully admit how they feel. They might be trying to avoid something, or because they literally might be flat out lying to themselves and to you to avoid a particular experience. Which is fine. But during this Full Moon in Gemini, expect for the truth to come out.

Full Moons represent culmination and release. They illuminate what has been hidden, and this one illuminates conversations, assumptions, and unspoken feelings. And babe, don't be surprised if the truth is literally the opposite of what you were thinking, because that's Gemini.

Plot twists and sudden truths. The Moon's connection with Uranus, the planet of surprises, means revelations arrive sideways—through a message, a comment, a confession you did not see coming. You thought your crush didn't actually like you? Turns out they did like you. They just had a repressively interesting way of showing it!

This Full Moon is happening while we are rising in Capricorn energy, and the Moon falls into the house of pleasure and creativity, which is why we're going to have some interesting truths come out. For example, you may think that you were ghosted because somebody didn't like you. No, babes—you were ghosted because they sincerely didn't like themselves, and that was the truth. And I would recommend not being all that surprised if you notice somebody circling back into your life. That is just the nature of this celestial event.

Don't play the authority. With the Moon making some pretty tough aspects to Mars and Jupiter, this is not the time to act as though you are the authority and know it all. This is a Moon that is going to cause you to pause, to stop, to reflect, and then to decide if you actually even want to engage. With the Moon in Gemini making a square to both Jupiter and Mars in Leo, any bold and flamboyant acts of arrogance are likely to be met with unpleasant consequences.

Especially because Mars, the planet of drive and motivation, and Jupiter, the planet of expansion and knowledge, are conjunct the South Node, which basically translates into this: you might think that you are sure of something, but you will find that having a big and loud and even aggressive way of explaining it makes you look like the problem. Try to avoid the post-shadow cringe! The best way to handle this transit is actually to pull back, to detach, and really take more of an observant perspective. Did you really fully understand the nuances of what somebody was going through? And if you liked them, did you really feel like you liked them, or did you like the time shared with them? Because people come in layers, babe, and that doesn't mean that you have to wait for a whole decade to know someone. But I'm just here to remind you that knowing someone for a decade doesn't really mean that you know them completely. Focus on letting life expose itself to you, as opposed to feeling as though you are the authority on what life is.

A liar's moon. The Moon is making an interesting sextile to Neptune in Aries, which means that, again, this is a liar's moon. And again, that does not mean that people are lying with malicious intent, but it means that things are just simply not clear. So sit back, be patient, and give life a little bit of time to unfold.You might be thinking that somebody's given up on you, or you might feel like you are wasting your time, saying things like "if they wanted to, they would." Well, babes, let me tell you: life is so much more complicated than instant gratification.

Read your Sun sign to gain insight into the version of yourself that you’re maturing into. Read your Rising sign to reflect on your present state. Review your Moon sign for guidance on how to nurture your inner self (no one is perfect). If you find your horoscope helpful, please share it and tag me on social media @monishaholmes.

This Full Moon lights up your house of communication, so the plot twist arrives as a message, a comment, or something said almost in passing. Something you were sure you understood about a friend, a sibling, or a situationship flips on its head. Your instinct is to fire back fast and loud—don't. With the Moon squaring Mars, your ruler, the loudest response is the one that backfires. Observe first. The full story is still loading.

Moon lesson: You don't have to respond to every revelation the second it lands.

Your house of money and values is front and center, and this Moon is asking what you thought you knew about your own worth. News about money, a rate, or what you're being offered could turn out to be the opposite of the story you were telling yourself—in either direction. Somebody may also finally admit what they actually value about you. Don't rush to react. Let the real number, and the real feeling, reveal itself.Moon lesson: What you thought something was worth—including you—is being repriced.

This is your Full Moon, so the truth surfacing is about you. Something you've been telling yourself—about what you feel, what you want, who you're becoming—is ready to be revised. That might sting for a second, but this is a gift, not an exposure: you're the sign of the plot twist, and this one frees you. Someone could circle back with a confession, too. Listen more than you explain. You don't have to narrate yourself perfectly right now.Moon lesson: The plot twist is you—and that's a gift, not an exposure.

Your house of rest and release is lit up, which means the truths arriving now come quietly: in dreams, in the shower, in the moment before sleep. Someone from the past may be circling your thoughts, and the full story of why things went the way they did is softer than the version you've been carrying. You don't have to act on any of it yet. Some truths are just for you first.Moon lesson: Not every truth needs an audience yet—some are just for you first.

Mars and Jupiter are in your sign, squaring this Full Moon in your house of friendship and community—so please, do not perform certainty in the group chat right now. A friend could reveal something that flips what you assumed about them completely, and the loud, flamboyant response is the one that embarrasses you later. Pull back and watch. The people in your life come in layers, and this Moon is showing you a new one.

Moon lesson: Being the loudest in the room is not the same as being right.

This Moon lands in your house of career and public life, and a narrative about your work or your reputation could flip without warning. Your urge will be to correct the record immediately, with receipts. Resist it. Things are genuinely unclear right now—this is a liar's moon, and nobody has the full picture, including the people talking. Let the story settle before you decide what actually needs your response.

Moon lesson: Let the record correct itself before you rush to defend it.

Your house of travel, learning, and big beliefs is activated, and a long-distance conversation—or a perspective from someone who sees the world completely differently—could flip a belief you've held for a long time. That's not a loss. That's the whole point of this Moon. Venus, your ruler, is in your sign keeping the delivery graceful, so let your mind actually change. Certainty is not the flex right now; curiosity is.

Moon lesson: Changing your mind is not losing—it's learning out loud.

Mercury, the messenger, is in your sign, digging underneath everyone's polite version of events, and this Full Moon lights up your house of intimacy and shared resources. A partner, an ex, or someone close could finally say the real thing: why they pulled away, what they were actually feeling, what they couldn't admit. It may be the opposite of what you assumed. You were probably never ghosted because of you, babe.

Moon lesson: The truth about why they went quiet was never really about you.

The Sun is in your sign, directly across from this Full Moon in your house of partnership—so the revelation comes through a person. A partner, a close friend, or even an opponent says the opposite of what you expected, and your first instinct is to lecture. Don't play the authority. Listening is the flex. You may find you liked the idea of someone more than the reality, or the reverse. Both are useful truths.

Moon lesson: Listening is the flex right now—you don't have to win the conversation.

This Full Moon rises with your sign setting the tone, and it lands in your house of work and daily routines. The flip here is practical: a coworker dynamic, a workload truth, or something about your own habits that you've been avoiding admitting. You don't need one big dramatic overhaul. Small, honest adjustments—including admitting you're tired, or that a system isn't working—will move more than force will.

Moon lesson: Small honest adjustments beat one big dramatic overhaul.

This is the fun one—the Full Moon lands in your house of pleasure, creativity, and romance, which is exactly where this lunation's juiciest truths live. A crush could confess the opposite of what you assumed. Someone could circle back. A creative project could reveal a direction you weren't expecting. Let it be interesting. Just remember the observer rule: watch how they show up over time before you decide what it means.

Moon lesson: Let them circle back—then decide if the door is actually open.

Your house of home and family is lit up, and a family truth could surface now—something a relative finally admits, or something about your home life that turns out to be different than the story you've been telling. Things at home are genuinely a little foggy under this Moon, so don't force the big conversation tonight. Give it time to unfold. Patience is how this one resolves.

Moon lesson: Home truths take time to land—you don't have to solve them tonight.

#7003. The Watches of Grand Theft Auto 6 (and Their Real-Life Inspiration)

From Rolex Day-Dates to Casio digitals, a guide to the timepieces of Vice City.

For one glorious moment, the world appears to be united. Grand Theft Auto VI has finally arrived, to near-universal acclaim, with millions of people abandoning work, school, and presumably all their other responsibilities to explore the streets of Vice City. In these divided times, it's enough to warm the cockles of your heart.

And amid all the fast cars, guns, nightclubs, and criminal capers, there's something else we pay attention to here at Esquire HQ: the watches!

Rockstar Games' latest blockbuster reportedly features 34 "collectible" luxury watches scattered throughout its fictional version of Florida. But that's only part of the story. GTA VI also has its own watch brands, although some of the designs look ... suspiciously familiar.

There's Crowex (a dead ringer for Rolex), Takio (distinctly Casio-adjacent), and Covgari, whose watches owe more to Audemars Piguet than the Italian-o jeweller its name suggests.

From gold Day-Dates to pocket-money Casio digitals, here are some of our favorite real-world watches that appear to have inspired the game's timepieces.

The gold Crowex is a near dead ringer for the Rolex Day-Date 40 (ref. 228238). The fluted bezel, black dial, and day window at 12 o'clock are all present and correct, although the bracelet differs somewhat from Rolex's iconic President. The Day-Date celebrates its 70th anniversary this year. Evidently, it remains the universal symbol of having made it, even in the virtual world.

The silver chronograph worn by a character overlooking Vice City has more than a passing resemblance to the mighty Daytona. The round case, contrasting sub-dials, and metal bracelet suggest that famous racing chronograph. As we know, the Daytona is one of the world's most sought-after sports watches, making it a natural fit for GTA's world of expensive cars, conspicuous consumption, and bling.

Brian Heder, a veteran drug smuggler, wears a metal sports watch that certainly seems to recall Rolex's Sub. There's the substantial case, dark dial, and the bracelet. The Submariner has been a fixture of luxury watchmaking since 1953, and its more ostentatious steel-and-gold versions would be right at home on the streets of Vice City.

Jason Duval, one of GTA VI's two protagonists, wears a chunky digital watch resembling something from Casio's Pro Trek range. The large circular display, prominent buttons and rugged case certainly suggest its something from the range of the Japanese juggernaut. A more practical choice than the gold watches favored by some of his associates.

Not everyone in Vice City is wearing a gold Rollie lookalike. The Takio F-BLAST is an unmistakable nod to Casio's inexpensive digital classics, particularly the bestselling F-91W. It has the familiar rectangular display, black dial surround, and simple, functional design. There's even a more colorful version, with a red case and two-tone metal bracelet, spotted in the game's Hamlet Thrift store.

As spotted by watch Instagram account @geesgshock.

#6995. ‘The Gilded Age’ Just Used a TV Trope We Really Need to Retire

Spoilers for The Gilded Age season 4 ahead.

Oh, Marian. Marian, Marian, Marian. We finally got to see her letting loose, and then it all came crashing down. Hard. As you may have seen coming from a mile away, on Sunday night’s fourth episode of The Gilded Age season 4, Marian realized that she was pregnant (at least she assumes she is). After a one-night stand. I think I’ve seen this show before—and it was created and written by the same person.

The season 4 premiere shows Marian Brook going out on the town with her ex-fiancé Larry Russell following Enid and Oscar’s wedding. They have a great time dancing, and then they have a great time at a hotel after bad weather means they can’t make the trip back uptown. Marian literally lets her Victorian-era hair down and loses her virginity to the man she loves—or at least, loved.

The aftermath is not fun, though. Marian thinks of herself as a ruined woman for having premarital sex, and Larry offers to get re-engaged to her—a fine idea considering they both still have feelings for each other—but she sees it as a pity re-proposal and refuses him. A few episodes later, she’s having nosebleeds all over the place and admitting to a colleague at the settlement house that she hasn’t had her “monthly courses.” (We’ll get to more on why I don’t like this storyline, but I will give the show a shoutout for using the underrepresented pregnancy symptom of nosebleeds rather than the classic barfing everywhere.)

This could have been different. Marian, a character who has had happiness snatched out of her hands time and time again, could have just stubbornly continued to turn down Larry and devoted herself to her work, only to realize by the season finale that she actually is still in love with him. I wouldn’t have minded a good old slow burn here! Much of this show moves so fast. Marian and Larry’s romance could’ve kept things more grounded.

But, instead, we get the classic TV trope of “woman gets pregnant after having sex with someone once.” And this particular situation has the added bonus of “and she was a virgin.” Shows as wide-ranging as Friends, Mad Men, The Secret Life of the American Teenager, Reign, Ginny & Georgia, and The Originals have featured one-night stands that lead to pregnancies to varying levels of success. The trope is supposed to make us gasp, but at this point, it’s just kind of cheap and predictable.

Most notably, this already happened on Downton Abbey, which, like The Gilded Age, was written and created by Julian Fellowes. The character Edith—who is very similar to Marian in that she repeatedly gets into doomed relationships with men and devotes herself to work at a time when that’s not the norm for aristocratic women—gets pregnant after sleeping with her love interest, Michael Gregson, one time. As with The Gilded Age, given the time period of the show, this was not happy news and was just another blow to a character who kept getting the short end of the stick. (Speaking of the short end of the stick, Michael Gregson goes out of the country following their lovemaking and dies while away, so Edith never even gets to see him again!)

The whole “Poor Edith” thing became something of a running joke for Downton fans and on the actual show, which was entertaining, but seeing something so similar happen again on The Gilded Age just makes me wish this had gone in a more creative direction. Maybe Marian could’ve kept hooking up with Larry despite the side of herself that knew it was a bad idea. Maybe Marian could’ve sworn off of men altogether. (Honestly, it’d make sense at this point! I mean, damn, that’s three failed engagements!) Anything but pregnancy.

So, where does Poor Marian go from here? My guess, it’ll either be: 1. She isn’t actually pregnant, but the stress from her guilt caused the nosebleeds and missed “monthly courses.” 2. She has the baby and secretly puts it up for adoption a la Edith from Downton. 3. She gets back with Larry, has the baby, and Bertha does some of her conniving to get society to not ask questions about the timeline. 4. She has a miscarriage.

Are any of these options that great? Is this going to be worth it? A miscarriage would mean it was just for the shock (or, “shock”) of the episode 4 cliffhanger. A baby with Larry would be forcing them together more than the guilt of the one-night stand.

Despite my critique of this particular storyline, I love this show, so I am interested to see how it turns out. The creativity might not have been there when it came to using this trope again, but hopefully, it’ll be there in the outcome.

#6990. So Many Dyson Hair Tools Are on Sale Right Now—These Are Cosmo Editors’ 6 Favorites

4 beauty editors. 6 Dyson tools. Here’s our official ranking.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

As we haircare lovers all know, Dyson has truly solidified itself as the crème de la crème of buzzy hair tools. From the viral Airwrap i.d. to the OG Supersonic Hair Dryer, Dyson = high-tech hairstyling. Despite the higher price points, these tools are everywhere these days, from your favorite creator doing their hair on your TikTok feed to your hairstylist doing your hair in the salon chair. It seems like everyone loves a Dyson product (and that includes pretty much every Cosmo editor too). They’ve won tonssss of Cosmo beauty awards, including a 2025 Holy Grail Beauty Award and 2026 Reader’s Choice Beauty Award for the Airwrap, back-to-back wins in the Reader’s Choice Beauty Awards in 2024 and 2025 for the Supersonic, and many, many more.

However, at $250-$500 a pop, it’s not like most people can afford to own every single Dyson tool out there. So which ones are the most worth the hundreds of dollars they cost? Luckily, we’ve here at Cosmo have tested all of ‘em—these are the ones we truly recommend spending money on.

Why we love it: Despite so many Airwrap dupes out there, us Cosmo editors keep going back to the original Dyson hair styling tool that changed the game forever. Talk about true innovation—the Airwrap created a whole new category of styling products that dry, curl, volumize, straighten, and blow out hair all in one handy tool that, oh by the way, uses hot air instead of high heat that could potentially damage your hair more.

It’s also on rare sale right now—23 percent off—for Black Friday and Cyber Monday, so if you’ve been thinking of scooping one up, this is your time to shine. Run, don’t walk!

One Cosmo editor says: “If the Airwrap has zero fans, I’m dead. For years, I didn’t know how to style my hair. The round-brush-and-dryer combo was a lot for me, I didn’t know how to curl my hair, and quite honestly, all I ever did was straighten it with a flat iron (which was perfect for the mid-2010s style at the time). But when I got my hands on my first Airwrap, that all changed. Suddenly, I could do it all, and I was imparting wayyy less heat damage onto my strands than if I had been using traditional hot tools. The i.d. is great because the it has all the attachments I need, it dries and styles my thick, wavy hair super quickly, it eliminates frizz, and keeps my style in place all day.” —CATHARINE MALZAHN, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY WRITER

Why we love it: The one that started it all and turned hair dryers from a humble styling tool into a must-have item... Dyson’s Supersonic dryer launched 10 years ago in 2016, yet it still remains one of the best hair dryers out there. And honestly, it’s not hard to see why. Beyond the basics you’d be looking for (long cord, negative ions, multiple heat and speed settings, relatively lightweight, that kind of thing), its powerful airflow dries hair noticeably quickly while the dryer monitors the temperature 40 times a second to prevent extreme heat on your strands.

It’s pretty straightforward and easy to use, making a salon-worthy blowout at home feel a whole lot less intimidating. A decade later, the Supersonic is proof that some beauty investments really do stand the test of time.

One Cosmo editor says: “This is the first Dyson hair tool I ever used, and I still think it's probably the most worth-it tool price-wise if you wanted to splurge on one thing. I used to hate blow dryers until I tried this one. It's quick and powerful without sacrificing the health of my hair. This fully dries my hair in under two minutes (yes, two), but it doesn't leave my hair crunchy or fried either.” —BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Straightening without hot plates? And you can use it on wet hair? Sign me up. The Dyson Airstrait uses airflow to dry and straighten hair at the same time, meaning you can skip the blow-dryer-and-flat-iron combo entirely. Perfect if you’re trying to cut down on styling time in the morning!

Unlike traditional flat irons, the Airstrait doesn’t use heated plates. Instead, it uses airflow to smooth and dry hair, delivering a soft, naturally straight finish with movement rather than that super-flat, pin-straight look. Plus, it has a dry mode for touching up your style between washes. When our editors with varying curl patterns (from wavy and fine to 4b curls) tested it, it straightened with ease and speed.

One Cosmo editor says: “I have an interesting hair type because while it’s curly and fine, I have a lot of it. It can take a very long time for me to dry my hair, but the Airstrait cut my styling time dramatically. This tool takes my hair from damp to fully dry in under 10 minutes. However, it did take a slight learning curve at first.” —MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY WRITER

Why we love it: Meet the Dyson hair dryer that had three Cosmo beauty editors with three different hair types completely obsessed. The Dyson Supersonic r takes everything we love about the original Supersonic and makes it lighter, smaller, and easier to maneuver, thanks to its curved, ergonomic design. In fact, it’s so lightweight that it weighs less than a pound, making it perfect for professional stylists as well as anyone who doesn’t want an arm workout just to dry hair.

Speaking of which, your arms probably won’t get super tired because of how quickly it works. Beauty director Lauren Balsamo said it dried her soaking-wet hair in just six minutes(!!!). Add in the intelligent attachments that automatically adjust heat and airflow and remember those settings for the next time you use it, the multiple attachments, and the compact size, and it’s easy to be willing to overlook the hefty price tag.

One Cosmo editor says: “The first thing I noticed when I picked up the Supersonic R was the weight, or rather, the lack thereof. It seriously feels like you're holding a bottle of hairspray—that’s how airy and easy it is to handle. Then came the assembly. The magnetic attachments snapped on effortlessly. And as a wavy-haired girl who can either lean curly or reach for a round brush for a bouncy blowout, I really appreciated the range of attachments for everything from creating curls to smoothing flyaways.” —JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Dyson finally made a one-step blow dryer brush, and at $250, it’s the brand’s lowest-priced hair tool (though it’s still an investment!). Like the Airwrap, the Dyson Airsmooth is another wet-to-dry styler, using powerful airflow to dry and smooth strands without extreme heat. Its teardrop shape helps you get closer to the root for more lift and volume, while the bristles are designed to minimize snagging, pulling, or breakage. It can smooth thick, wavy hair in under 15 minutes, leaving it sleek, shiny, and noticeably less frizzy.

If you’re shopping for a new blow dryer brush, this is a great pick. It’s perfect for anyone who loves a smooth, bouncy blowout but doesn’t want all the bells and whistles of an Airwrap.

One Cosmo editor says: “I was thrilled to find out Dyson had created a one-stop tool for drying and styling, complete with upgraded bristle technology. I want to preserve as many of my precious strands as possible, and what I love most about this tool (besides the low-for-Dyson price) is that its bristles aren’t your basic plastic teeth that tug at your hair. Instead, they bend with your strands as you pull the brush through, releasing them rather than fighting against the tension. I usually finish styling with a solid clump of hair left behind on my tool, but with the Airsmooth, there were truly fewer than five strands caught in the bristles.” —JH

Why we love it: An update from the iconic and original Supersonic, this even-more-high-tech dryer features a Scalp Protect mode that uses sensors to detect how close the tool is to your head and automatically adjusts the heat to help maintain a scalp temperature of approximately 131°F. Translation: You can dry your roots without blasting your scalp with so much heat that you flinch when your dryer gets too close.

It also features intelligent attachments that remember your preferred settings, plus a Pause Detect function that reduces airflow when you put the dryer down. And, of course, you still get the powerful, fast-drying performance Dyson is known for.

One Cosmo editor says: “I have the Supersonic at my parents' house (I gifted my old one to my mom a few years ago), but the Nural is at mine. This is the more high-tech version of the Supersonic, which automatically shifts air flow and heat based on how close the tool is to your scalp. If blow dryers often feel like they're burning off your scalp, this one will turn that around.” —BG

Dyson hair tools are definitely not one-size-fits-all, and what works wonders on someone’s fine, straight hair might not do the same for thick curls or coils.

Before dropping hundreds of dollars on a Dyson, ask yourself: What do I actually want this thing to do? If you’re constantly switching up your hairstyle, the Airwrap’s versatility might justify the splurge. But if your goal is simply to dry your hair quickly, you probably don't need all those attachments—a Supersonic will do the trick. Love a sleek, straight finish? Try the Airstrait. Simply consider which tools you’d actually see yourself using every day before taking the plunge.

Short answer: Heck yeah! There’s a reason Dyson hair tools have earned cult-favorite status among us beauty editors, hairstylists, and pretty much everyone on TikTok. We’ve tested practically every Dyson out there, and we’re consistently impressed. For thick, long, or doesn’t-want-to-stay-styled hair, these can be especially worth the money.

Of course, there’s no denying that they’re a serious investment. So if you’re looking for a budget alternative, there are plenty of options out there too. But in our experience, the Dyson’s quick-drying capabilities sans excessive heat damage, customizable settings, and long-lasting results make them worth the splurge—especially if you’re someone who styles their hair regularly. And while you certainly don't need to own every Dyson tool (your bank account can thank us later), finding the right one for your hair type and styling goals can be a total game changer.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s written about all things hair, from cool hair color trends to the best haircuts worth trying. She’s an avid Airwrap user and has tested several versions of the hair styler over the years.

#6988. Thom Browne and Mr Porter Just Released an Exclusive New Capsule Collection

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The 17-piece lineup makes its debut on young restaurateur George McNally.

Launching this week on Mr Porter is a 17-piece capsule collection created with New York’s own Thom Browne. Browne is famed in men’s fashion for his conceptual runway shows—staged in Florence, Milan, Paris and, of course New York—but beneath all the elevated showmanship lies a broad and distinctly wearable aesthetic.

Consisting of casual and tailored pieces and extending to luggage, home goods, and even the recently launched Thom Browne x Bose headphones, the collection puts a youthful twist on Browne’s signature style that blends sportswear and suits with a rigorous attention to detail. Browne has acted as Chairman of the Council of Fashion Designers of America since 1923 and won its prestigious Menswear Designer award four times in his 25-year career.

To showcase the collaboration, the capsule was photographed by Ian Kenneth Bird on young New York restaurateur George McNally. If the name McNally rings a bell, that’s no accident; his surname carries a lot of weight in the New York food scene. George is the son of Keith (Balthazar, Minetta Tavern) and nephew of Brian (Indochine), both past masters of creating downtown Manhattan restaurants with an enduring buzz in a notoriously fickle foodie scene.

George—who, at just 23, has worked pretty much every restaurant position going in New York and London since he was a teenager—is tracing his own culinary path with Faux, his hot new space in Tribeca, serving reasonably priced, loosely French-Mediterranean food, and a space that stays open late for a city that, proverbially, never sleeps.

Go to Mr Porter to see and shop the collection and read George’s accompanying interview.

#6986. Is Social Media Really the Great Equalizer? Here’s What Hulu’s ‘The Circle’ Host Tinx Has to Say (Exclusive)

I sat down with Christina Najjar to talk alliances, authenticity, and the responsibility of being the “internet’s big sister.”

After a seven-season run on Netflix, The Circle has found a new home on Hulu, and things look very, very different. Rather than “normies,” the cast is now made up of familiar faces from the Walt Disney Company reality TV ecosystem and micro-influencers disguised as civilians. (Even the player with the most modest initial following, Cyrus Irani, had over 10,000 followers at the time his casting was announced. Is it a problem? Not necessarily. But it’s certainly a switch-up from season 1’s social media skeptic Shubham Goel.)

The revival series is also airing in near-real time, with new episodes dropping Tuesday through Thursday and audience voting being used to shape the process. And perhaps the biggest change: stepping into hosting duties in place of comedian and actor Michelle Buteau is SiriusXM’s Christina “Tinx” Najjar, who rose to fame on social media during the COVID-19 pandemic.

As a longtime Circle devotee, I was...curious about what these changes might mean for my favorite competition series. So when I got the chance to fly to Atlanta to visit the set (and meet Tinx) myself, I took it. There I chatted with her about the power of social media, the potential problem of having celebs in the Circle, and the player she thinks might win it all.

I truly couldn’t believe it. I know how many loyal fans The Circle has, so when I got the call that I got it—I mean, I’m still pinching myself every day. It’s a really big moment for me, and I know I have big shoes to fill. And I really just want to make everybody proud.

I remember watching the very first episode and thinking, “Wow, this is such a fresh concept, and it’s truly a social experiment for the modern age.” So the fact that I’m here all these years later is truly a pinch-me moment.

I relate to it so much, because in COVID, I was living in a studio apartment in L.A. I had just moved. I didn’t have a lot of friends, and all I had was my phone. That’s why I initially picked up TikTok and thought, “Who knows what’s going to happen in the world? I might as well make a few videos and try to make friends online.” And as soon as I started creating content, I just realized, “Wow, I love connecting with people. I love entertaining people.” It’s been a wild six years ever since.

I grew up doing theater in high school, but honestly, no. I truthfully had a lot of limiting beliefs about myself. I just thought there was no way I could ever be an influencer. I didn’t feel pretty enough. I didn’t think I was put together enough. And I think one thing COVID taught us is that anybody who has a dream can go for it. Social media is really the great equalizer in that sense, because if you are funny, if you have something to say, you can get online and you can connect with people.

It’s such an honor, and I love that people look up to me for advice. It’s the honor of my life that people trust me enough to call me on my radio show for advice or write to me, and I just try to be a voice of reason and remind women to know their worth. I feel like this is a journey I was meant to be on, and all of the opportunities that being online has afforded me, because it really has given me so much. The fact that I’m sitting here as the host of The Circle is—again, I can’t believe it.

It’s so different because I’m used to just holding up my phone, recording, cutting quickly, and pressing post. This is a totally different operation, but the crew is so cool, production is so cool, and the writers that I work with are just so smart and so funny. I was actually shocked when they explained to me the process of how close to the wire we are. It is as close to real time as you could ever think.

Because I have lived online for six years, I post in real time myself. I’m someone who wakes up and I’m like, “Hey guys, I’m going to the gym. Here’s what I’m eating for breakfast.” I don’t know if I would know how to do it any other way, because I’m just, like, an oversharer, so I think I would just be myself and see what happened. But I can be strategic. I feel like I would make a lot of alliances. I’m good at that.

I honestly think maybe the celebrities are at a slight disadvantage because, as you say, people have perceptions about them. I feel like they also have more of a target on their backs.

He was shocked. He had such a good strategy. I was honestly shocked he got out, too. He’s so likable, and everyone knew it was really him. He got got. He got got by Justin [Hinsley], if I’m being honest, who has very strong strategy. I think Justin is incredibly strategic and knows how to play the game.

I mean, Hannah [Brown], obviously. I don’t think she knows how to play any other way. She’s so genuine, and she’s definitely playing with her heart. So we’ll see how that fares and whether it’s strategy or heart that wins.

I think it’s totally fine, because there’s a lot on the line here. This is a popularity contest, but there’s a big prize at the end. So I think it’s fun when people are ruthless. Why not? It’s a game, and games are meant to be won.

#6984. 12 Watches That Make a Great Gift for the Man in Your Life

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you’re on a Panerai budget or something closer to Casio.

A watch is a sentimental gift. It’s not something casually given—not a tie or a fancy notebook or even a good pen or a nice bottle of booze. Don’t get me wrong, those are delightful things to receive and all appropriate for a run-of-the-mill holiday or birthday celebration. But some years aren’t run-of-the-mill. Some years require something extra. Some years are watch years—and trust me, you’ll know it when you get there.

But what watch? It’s a personal choice, of course, but a personal choice that you’re making for someone else. Luckily, you likely know them pretty well. Around these parts, Esquire knows watches pretty well. Here are 12 suggestions, in price ranges from midwestern frugal to multigeneration inheritance. No matter how much you’re comfortable spending, you can’t go wrong with any of these watches.

A classic watch for a classic guy. The Khaki Field Expedition is the quintessential field watch. Rich history of American craftsmanship, purpose-driven aesthetics, functional compass bezel (even if I can’t figure out how to use it for the life of me), and in the past few years it’s been available with the always practical NATO strap. It’s an ideal watch for the horologist, who’ll appreciate the history, the movement, and the exhibition case back. Win-win, guaranteed all-star gift.

I’m an unabashed Citizen fan. The Promaster “Fugu” is my favorite watch in the $500 range, and the Japanese brand’s Eco-Drive offerings are even more affordable. The Promaster Sea Eco-Drive is almost criminally underpriced for the watch that you get. We’re talking an emerald-green dial with a matching unidirectional 60-minute bezel, 200 meter depth rating, and a stainless-steel bracelet for well under $300. This is a great gift for the practically driven, classic dive-watch fan. And if the green dial is too swerve-y, the black and the blue are both excellent choices.

Good doesn’t always mean expensive. A big ol’ dive watch isn’t appropriate for every occasion or every guy. Simpler can be better. Like this under-$100 life-proof G-Shock. It treads the line between classic G-Shock aesthetics and little Casio reference. A watch equally appropriate for a man who never thinks about watches or one who is always thinking about watches—Schrödinger’s timepiece of no taste or good taste. And, hot take, it pairs better with a suit than sneakers do. Keep your shoes formal and let your watch carry the high-low combo.

If he’s not a G-Shock man, he may be an Ironman. A Timex Ironman, that is, even if he’s not logging an insane amount of weekend mileage. As the world grows wary of increased technocracy-driven smart designs, there’s a simple nostalgia to the ’90s-tech runner’s watch. Turns out they were right all along. You don’t need a phone on your wrist, just a stopwatch and an alarm clock and a 100-lap memory to boot. I also like the orange accents here. If he is midwestern frugal and would object to a gift over $100, this scaled-back version also plays.

Shinola’s Duck Watch references ’60s and ’70s skin divers, which sounds dirty but really is just a term for smaller, less depth-rated dive watches and the culture of free diving and spearfishing around them. That’s a reductive explanation that some watch nerds will quibble with, but it’ll serve us for now. The two important things to take away are 1) there was a magazine of the same name that you can find on eBay and 2) Shinola’s modern version is a killer watch. I have this one in Canary Yellow, but I recently borrowed the new Mallard Brown colorway for a photo shoot, and I love it. Hated to send it back. In the sun, the dial turns almost iridescent, like the titular duck’s feathers. It’s battery powered, water-resistant, and not fussy at all.

[Editor’s note: The Mallard Brown colorway is currently out of stock, so for the time being we will direct you to the Canary.]

Let’s get fancy. But let’s get fancy with purpose. More on Rolexes soon, but I think Panerai makes the most exciting watches in the $5,000 to $15,000 range. I realize that’s a large range, one encompassing the price of a decent motorcycle or a used Camry, but I’m talking luxury Swiss-made watches here. This is the tip of the iceberg. Panerai has design panache and exquisite movements, a design that’s not too “look at me” but still far enough outside the classic mold that it demands a second look. It’s not a watch for everyone, but man, those who will love it will love it immensely.

Recently I hosted a friend and fellow style writer for a will-not-be-named rival publication at the Detroit headquarters of T-shirt maker Soft Goods MFG. He had a couple enviable pieces (understated Nitty Gritty corduroy jacket, Paraboot derbies, perfectly faded vintage Levi’s), but I kept looking at his watch, a Unimatic Quattro. In the interest of individuality and my own love of a good suede, I’m suggesting this Todd Snyder collab as the next gift for that extra-special man in your life. It’s Unimatic with a twist, Italian design with an Americana POV, like spaghetti western for your wrist. Clint Eastwood fans rejoice.

Timex and J. Crew’s recent trout-forward collab watch sold out in an instant—unfortunately before I could get my hands on one. But there are other options for the outdoorsy dude. Especially one who cares about stopping forest fires. This Vero Smokey Bear option toes the line between campy and classy with aplomb. It’ll look good while you’re chopping wood or a little Wes Anderson–y at the lodge with a glass of bourbon in hand, telling stories about the trout that got away.

TAG can get a bad rap in the watch-collecting community, but ignore the critics, especially if your intended recipient is a racing enthusiast. TAG Heuer has a long history in F1 and Grand Prix, and it looks as much at home in the cockpit of a fancy Brad Pitt–piloted car as it does in the fan stands or the sports bar. If you want to step it up a level, the Monaco is a luxury take on a race-day watch—all European angles and rich leather.

This is the only pilot’s watch on the list—and for good reason. It’s the most handsome pilot’s watch on the market. Marathon developed these in partnership with the Kelly Air Force Base in 1986 and specced it out to withstand high-altitude flight, or even an ejection. If you’re in the market for a classic pilot’s chronograph, Bremont makes the best one. But if you are in the market for a no-frills, all-style, practically designed pilot’s watch, the Marathon Arctic Navigator is your ticket.

Maybe he’s a Rolex Pepsi guy, but you’re on a non-Swiss-made budget. I’ll let you in on a secret: Seiko makes a pretty damn good approximation. I particularly like that this one is PADI co-branded and automatic, with a 200 meter depth rating. Even better, it’s under $500.

For my money, Doxa makes some of the most interesting dive watches out there. Is this on the list because I’d really love if someone took the hint and bought one for me? Hard to say. What’s easy to say is that the orange-dial Doxa Sub 200T is a showstopper. It’s got a slightly smaller profile and a more interesting silhouette than the original 200 but still retains the necessary details, like its unidirectional rotating bezel with its integrated U.S. Navy no-decompression limit table. No rules on whether that’s used underwater or just at the dinner table, either. It’s good to know how long to cook a steak for.

If you’ve ever thought Beyoncé has good hair, Cécred is partially why. Co-created with her mother and longtime hairstylist Tina Knowles, Cécred is a full line for all hair types, but many of the products are strengthening and nourishing for any kind of dry, damaged hair. At initial launch, Cécred started with the Foundation Collection, featuring a Clarifying Shampoo & Scalp Scrub, Hydrating Shampoo, Moisturizing Deep Conditioner, Reconstructing Treatment Mask, Fermented Rice & Rose Protein Ritual, Moisture Sealing Lotion, and the Nourishing Hair Oil. But the hero was the Fermented Rice and Rose Protein Ritual: a two-step system that uses fermented rice, amino acids, and biotin to help fortify hair strands and repair damage.

But since its launch in 2024, Cécred has expanded with a whole line of stylers, the viral Restoring Hair and Edge Drops, and more.

And it’s not just another cash-grab celebrity brand, by the way. Cécred’s won multiple Cosmo beauty awards, including best hair game changer in our 2024 Holy Grail Beauty Awards and best hair oil for the Nourishing Hair Oil. We named the Moisturizing Deep Conditioner the best overall deep conditioner last year, and the brand is #6 best celebrity beauty brand (ahead of 30+ others).

#6976. Saatva’s Luxury Mattresses Are 20 Percent Off for Black Friday—Only With Our Exclusive Code

A better night’s sleep starts with your mattress, and readers can save up to $1,000 right now.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE Decor editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Saatva’s Black Friday sale is here early for ELLE Decor readers. From November 6 through December 1, you can save 20 percent on your purchase—as long as your cart includes a mattress. Depending on the model and size, you could save up to $1,000. Whether you’re getting the guest room ready for holiday visitors or considering a luxury mattress upgrade for your primary bedroom, there’s no better time to invest in your sleep.

To take advantage of our exclusive offer, shop through our links below and the discount will automatically be applied at checkout.

I’ve tested many mattresses, and Saatva’s Classic Mattress remains my favorite. It balances plush cushioning with supportive lift, and in my experience, it feels even better than the mattresses I’ve slept on at luxury hotels. It’s worth the investment.

For a mattress designed to please every type of sleeper, opt for Saatva’s Classic style. It’s a sturdy mattress complete with a hybrid coils-meet-foam design. Available in three different firmness settings, you’ll get a plush, cloud-like feel from the cushioned foam top layer, which is set on top of pressure-relieving coils that contour to your body.

With my Luxury Firm mattress, I can feel myself sinking into the pillow top as soon as I lie down, while the supportive layers underneath help realign my body and take the pressure off my back and shoulders. I can comfortably sleep on my back or sides, and it has helped reduce body pains.

Beyond the popular Classic mattress, Saatva has a range of specialized models that cater to specific sleep concerns or lifestyle preferences, including mattresses made from organic latex and cooling memory foam options.

Perhaps Saatva’s most innovative model, the Rx mattress is specifically designed for those with chronic back or joint conditions like sciatica, arthritis, scoliosis, or a herniated disc. It has specialized lumbar zones for spinal alignment and extra support to alleviate pressure from the body.

For those who prefer an eco-conscious or hypoallergenic bed, Saatva’s Latex Hybrid and Zenhaven models are made from organic latex that is naturally cooling. The Zenhaven is the brand’s more premium option with dual-sided firmness (meaning you can flip it over when you’re craving a change).

The Memory Foam mattress is ideal for side sleepers, while the cooling qualities of the Contour5 make it a nice option for those who find memory foam hot. But if you’re sharing a bed with a partner who has different sleep preferences, opt for the Solaire, which has 50 firmness settings on each side, so no one has to compromise.

Take the brand’s online mattress quiz to help you make your decision.

Should you wind up unhappy with your Saatva mattress, the brand offers a 365-night home trial. If you’re not satisfied, you can return your mattress for a full refund or exchange it for another model within the trial period—just note that returns and exchanges are subject to a $99 pickup and processing fee.

Your mattress purchase comes with free white-glove delivery, which includes in-home setup and removal of your old mattress—so all you need to do is select which luxe bedding you want to add to it. Plus, Saatva’s mattresses are backed by a lifetime warranty, which covers any defects or repairs.

#6974. Brex: Field Marketing Coordinator, Upmarket

Brex is the intelligent finance platform that enables companies to spend smarter and move faster in more than 200 markets. By combining global corporate cards and banking with intuitive spend management, bill pay, and travel software, Brex enables founders and finance teams to accelerate operations, gain real-time visibility, and control spend effortlessly. Tens of thousands of the world's best companies run on Brex, including DoorDash, Coinbase, Robinhood, Zoom, Plaid, Reddit, and SeatGeek.

Working at Brex allows you to push your limits, challenge the status quo, and collaborate with some of the brightest minds in the industry. We’re committed to building a diverse team and inclusive culture and believe your potential should only be limited by how big you can dream. We make this a reality by empowering you with the tools, resources, and support you need to grow your career.

#6973. Winter Essentials Worth Giving—and Keeping

Bring warmth and classic style to city days and outdoor adventures with gear from The North Face.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s an oft-expressed sentiment, but it’s true: The holidays turn people’s attention toward friends, family, and community. There’s a beauty in the selfless, caring gifts people give to others. And while all that giving feels great, the holidays are also a good time to treat yourself. Plus, it’s getting colder.

Whether you equate The North Face with everyday city wear or outdoor adventures, it’s a solid, time-tested spot to get a gift for a loved one (or yourself). The brand has jackets and accessories with familiar winter looks, as well as products with less expected silhouettes that still offer protection from the elements in an understated style.

Here’s a look at some of the best cold-weather gear that will have you wrapping up the gift of warmth and durability… even if that gift is for yourself.

This is an easy gifting win because the warm, hooded, insulated jacket is a recognizable The North Face staple, evoking an unpretentious spin on the brand’s iconic Nuptse Jacket.

It’s familiar, but it’s also practical—the kind of gift you feel good about because it’s going to get a ton of use. (And it can withstand the repeated wears.) The attached hood offers protection from biting winds, and the straightforward color options make it easy to pair with a variety of outfits, whether it’s something fun or part of a heavy bundling during the coldest days of winter. This is the perfect type of winter coat: warm but not too puffy.

The Zaneck Jacket is a great alternative to the Aconcagua, stepping away from the outdoorsy look the brand is best known for. It’s still waterproof and insulated, but it has a longer, more modern cut.

The straight cuffs add to its stylish feel in a way that doesn’t scream “I’m dressed for warmth and not fashion,” even though it’s doing both.

The Lone Peak Triclimate 2 has a lot in common with the Zaneck. From a distance, they cut a similar silhouette. However, the Lone Peak adds a little versatility if you’re looking for a jacket that can handle a variety of climates.

A removable, insulated inner jacket is attached to a removable, insulated top layer, which is fit for rainy conditions or as a spring or fall jacket. The insulated layer converts the whole thing into a winter-ready jacket. It also sports underarm vents capable of releasing heat in the middle of a long hike, ideal for outdoorsy types. The hood is removable and it’s got adjustable cuffs and an adjustable hem, making this a do-it-all jacket.

It might be easy to ignore the Pertex Jacket. It’s a bit unassuming. It doesn’t cut as stylish of a figure as the Zaneck, but it’s endlessly useful. The lightweight Pertex fabric will keep you dry in a downpour, but it also offers warmth without marshmallow-like bulk.

The lightweight aspect makes this the kind of coat you toss in the backseat on the way to a hike, just in case. It’s also great for layering, whether it’s tossed on over a sweater or layered under an overcoat to make a favorite jacket more winter-ready.

This edition of the classic Base Camp Duffel echoes the design details we love in The North Face’s jackets. While it’s got enough style and a heap of color options for daily use, it’s built for adventure.

Made with water- and tear-resistant fabric, it boasts 71 liters of storage space, a roomier option to the similar 31-liter version. Like all of the brand’s products, it’s built as if the designers have been through the rigors of outdoor adventures (and they probably have). It’s got a sweeping D-zip opening, backpack straps, mesh pockets, and compression straps in case you’re curious if you can pack 73 liters worth of gear into the 71-liter space. (You know you’ve tried.)

The North Face has a lot of hats, none more iconic than this. The Salty Lined Beanie has a classic feel, fitting snuggly over the head with rib-knit texture, a cuffed shape, soft fabric, and ample color options to ensure it will match any jacket or accessory. The Salty Lined Beanie encapsulates the entire The North Face vibe in a small way: It’s stylish enough for a subway ride to work and practical enough for an outdoor adventure. Best of all, it’ll keep your ears warm in either instance.

Often, the best glove is the one that disappears into the outfit. It looks seamless, keeping hands warm without drawing too much attention. The Etip Recycled Glove is about functionality first and foremost, but the simple design and understated colors makes it look good with a variety of winter outfits.

While we all like to pretend we aren’t attached to our phones, the Etip makes a great modern gift because its stretchy fleece delivers a snug fit and the palms have touchscreen compatibility. That means you won’t have to take off your gloves to respond to that important text while the snow is falling.

#6962. What Happened to “Cuffing Season?”

Is finding a holiday date really that important anymore?

In the early 2010s, the holiday season and “cuffing season” were basically synonymous. The dropping temperatures paired with the pressures of having to endure Thanksgiving dinner without a date seemed to usher in an urgency around dating. Cuffing season was the perfect time to find someone—even if it was just until the season was over.

But something has shifted. Cuffing season is nowhere near as prevalent as it used to be. Has it become yet another trend that’s just run its course—or is the decreased popularity a sign of something much larger happening in dating culture?

Relationship therapist Dr. Natalie Harvey explains that cuffing season hasn’t necessarily disappeared, but it has become fragmented. Dating apps and dating fatigue have changed the landscape of dating—including when people do it.

If you look at the numbers, people are still eager to couple up in the fall and winter. According to a survey by the Hily dating app, 79 percent of Gen Z and 65 percent of Millennial Americans want to find a partner to share the season with. But this doesn’t mean attitudes toward cuffing season are still positive. In the same survey, more than half of respondents said they believe people rush into relationships during cuffing season for the sake of comfort.

“There is less urgency, because the scarcity is gone,” Dr. Harvey says. “When you can meet someone in any month, winter loses its deadline power.”

She explains that there are other things at play as well. Dating is expensive, requires a lot of energy, and—most importantly—women’s attitudes toward relationships have shifted tenfold.

If we’re being totally transparent, cuffing season was essentially born out of a little bit of panic. People were so afraid to face fall and winter without a cuddle buddy that they decided to deem the season one of relationships. But views around dating have changed—especially with women in the dating pool.

“A lot of women have full, established lives that a seasonal relationship would disrupt rather than improve,” Dr. Harvey says. “The cold still makes companionship appealing—it just competes with more self knowledge than it used to.”

Vogue declared that having a boyfriend is embarrassing and we’ve been talking about the death of dating apps for years now. There are plenty of factors that feed into this (a failing economy, the rise of social media, the birth of red pill), but women play a pretty huge role in this shift.

“They have done the math on settling, and it never adds up,” Dr Harvey says.

She explains that women have their own incomes, homes, friendships, and entire lives they’ve built from the ground up. Having a man isn’t as “appealing” as it used to be, mostly because there’s no longer the sense of panic that cuffing season was based on.

“For a growing number of women, a partner has to add to their life, not just fill a seat at the holidays.”

Sure, cuffing season may not be as prevalent as it was before, but that doesn’t mean it has lost its charm entirely. Dr. Harvey explains that while the desire for partnership still exists, there are ways to use the season in a way that fueled by curiosity and reflection rather than hastiness.

“[Cuffing season] is still useful, but as a mirror, not a deadline,” she says. “Cuffing season is a good checkpoint to ask yourself what you actually want this winter.”

There’s nothing wrong with wanting to share the holidays with someone. Going to a pumpkin patch with a S.O., cuddling up with some hot chocolate and Christmas movies, having someone to kiss at midnight on New Year’s Eve—these are all fun and romantic things that are super fun with a date. But cuffing season should be about figuring out what you’re looking for in a partner—not just trying to find any “rando” to fill the void.

“If you do want to find a partner, decide your standards now—while you are clear—and do not renegotiate them in December,” she says. Don’t let cuffing season accelerate your search for a partner, either. There’s no point in partnering up for the holidays if you don’t really like who you’re dating.

Plus, dating has become so horrendous that partnering up just because there was a change in the season really feels like doing more harm than good. While the desire to partner up in the winter clearly still exists, there’s a general attitude that feels like people aren’t as pressed to follow through.

Cuffing season will always exist because people will always collectively want partnership. We’re just human. But the circumstances have changed significantly—and our requirements for who we date should adjust as well.

“Wanting love is healthy,” Dr. Harvey says. “Settling is just loneliness with a plus one.”

#6955. What’s a Vintage Car Rally Really Like? Inside the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run.

How I got mixed up in a race with a bunch of priceless historic automobiles and views of Italy’s spectacular Alpine scenery—and won.

In vintage rally driving, there are generally two types of competitor. Both share a passion for keeping priceless and nearly century-old vehicles in use on the open road rather than displaying them under glass. But some see the drive itself as challenge enough, while stalwarts adhere to what’s called the road book. This is a hundred or so pages of complex navigational directions that the copilot uses to navigate instead of a map, maintaining a constant flow of instructions to the driver. It is this book and how closely you follow it that decides the eventual winner.

Gianandrea Redaelli, a car nut whose family has long roots in the Italian printing business, is firmly in the “road book” camp. In early September, I climbed into the copilot seat of his 1939 Aston Martin C-Type Speed Model Roadster Bertelli—just one of two ever built, with an experimental aerodynamic body that earned it the nickname “the Flying Pea”—and stayed there as he drove six hundred kilometers of “left at the traffic lights, right at the cow” for the Louis Vuitton Dolomites Classic Run. The rally started in Venice and ended, after a scenic but challenging detour high into the Dolomite mountains, at Monza, the home of Italian Formula 1, just in time for practice day of the Grand Prix.

Venice might not seem the most logical starting line for a car rally given the proverbial wetness of the streets. But the city was chosen this year because it was also the end point of the last LV rally of its kind: the La Serenissima Run from Monte Carlo to Venice in 2012. Gianandrea won that one as copilot, with his father, Giuseppe, at the wheel. The pair also won the one before it—the Boheme across Hungary, the Czech Republic, and Austria—in 2006. This time, however, the pressure was on me too.

I have been a copilot before, and I can tell you it’s no picnic. Rally racing in vintage cars is less about speed and more about timing. It’s an exercise that requires pinpoint accuracy at the time-stamp points in the overall course and, most critically, in the regularities. These are fiendishly short time-over-distance sections in which crossing the line just a few hundredths of a second late—or early—earns strict penalties. Sensibly, the day before the race began, Gianandrea took me out in the Aston in the Veneto countryside to practice, especially those critical regularities.

I could have opted for an easy life. The rally allowed competing cars, dating from 1931 to 1969, to use the road book or follow the course using a map in a dedicated phone app. But where’s the fun in that?

“If you join a competition, you follow the regulations and strive to do your best,” Gianandrea says. “As an Italian, if you paint numbers on the door of my car, I will want to win.”

Every car in a rally like this comes with its own idiosyncrasies. On flat ground, someone driving a souped-up 2026 sports car would find these tests difficult to nail. In the reality of race conditions, the regularities often involve steep climbs or descents and unexpected corners, alling for an expert hand at the wheel—and foot on the gas pedal. The tendency of some car doors to swing open at hard bends (ours has the “suicide” doors with hinges on the back instead of the front, beloved by early carmakers) only adds to the excitement. The purpose of driver and copilot is to battle and overcome all the mechanical and computational challenges—and get our ride to the finish line bang on time.

Louis Vuitton has been involved in these glamorous and exacting vintage rallies since 1993. The Dolomites Run represents an enthusiastic return to the genre after a fourteen-year gap and, perhaps tellingly, the company’s global sponsorship (since 2025) of Formula 1. Unconfirmed rumors circulated in the field that the choice of ending the rally at a Grand Prix could well be repeated—and soon—in other countries in the future.

It’s easy, after all, to see why a fusion of style and cars with six- and seven-figure price tags might appeal to Louis Vuitton and competitors alike. Not surprisingly, at every coffee and lunch stop, the LV effect sprinkled the race with an added sense of luxury. In between, our snaking caravan carried the glamour of a century of motoring along with it. At every hairpin you got a glimpse of a gull-wing Mercedes, a 1931 Bugatti two-seater (my personal fave), and a shimmering behemoth of a Bentley in quick succession. These cars would turn heads anywhere. But in Italy, arguably the home of the sports car, they brought smiles, applause, and gasps in even the tiniest village that passed by in our slipstream. I swear I even saw a nun with a glint in her eye at one set of traffic lights.

Navigating, I was quickly learning, is not so much about providing a constant babble of information as it is knowing how to give clear and simple instructions—and only when strictly needed. As the finish neared in Monza at the end of two long days on the road, I was able to give Gianandrea the most helpful instruction of all: “Nothing for 15 kilometers.”

“That,” he very kindly replied, “is the mark of a pro.”

Even after the engines cooled, we were still clueless about how well we’d done overall. That changed at the final event, a dinner inside the Sforzesco Castle in Milan. Gianandrea stepped up, beaming, as winner of the race, and for the first time as driver. I stood somewhat sheepishly behind him as copilot as he received the trophy from Pietro Beccari, CEO of both Louis Vuitton and Louis Vuitton Fashion Group. It was an impressive swirl of Murano glass created by Venini, housed in its own equally impressive traveling case, made (naturellement) at Louis Vuitton’s atelier in Asnières.

Gianandrea has promised me visiting rights.

#6953. Julia Child’s Purple-Hued Cambridge Home Sells Just Days After Listing

The legendary TV chef’s Massachusetts house fetched $6.525 million—above the asking price.

In today’s real estate market, price cuts are increasingly common. But Julia Child’s former Cambridge home just bucked the trend, selling mere days after listing—and fetching above the asking price. The legendary TV chef’s longtime Massachusetts residence hit the market on September 23 for $6.475 million. By October 2, it had found a buyer. The sale officially closed on October 29, per Zillow. In the end, the six-bedroom Cape Cod–style house traded hands for $6.525 million, $50,000 above asking.

Child and her husband, Paul, lived in the house for decades, starting in 1961, after Paul retired from his job in the federal government. The estate is also where Child shot several popular TV shows, among them In Julia’s Kitchen with Master Chefs and Julia and Jacques Cooking at Home.

Painted a muted shade of purple, the 6,300-square-foot home sits on a quarter-acre lot just a few blocks from Harvard Yard. Dating to 1889, the residence at one time featured a custom kitchen designed especially for Child, with pegboard walls, a six-burner industrial Garland range, and maple countertops a few inches taller than average to suit her 6’3” frame.

In the wake of Paul’s 1994 death, Child continued to live in the residence until 2001, when she donated the property to Smith College, her alma mater. (The Massachusetts institution sold the home in 2002 for $2.35 million, per Zillow.) The famous kitchen, meanwhile, was meticulously deconstructed—down to its linoleum flooring—and rebuilt in D.C.’s National Museum of American History, where it remains on display today.

Since then, the new owners have revamped the three-story home’s kitchen, which now sports marble countertops, a metallic backsplash, Poggenpohl cabinetry, and a Sub-Zero refrigerator. The rest of the residence is equipped with light-filled interiors, hardwood floors, and eco-friendly additions like solar panels.

This summer, the Cambridge City Council passed a proposal to install a historical marker on the estate’s grounds, according to Boston.com.

By chance, another Child-linked property recently traded hands as well, albeit for a discount. In August, a Santa Barbara ranch where she filmed the 12-episode PBS series Dinner at Julia’s listed for just under $22 million. It sold on October 16 for $19 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.
