#6948. Is It Just Us? Or Was 2026 the Year of Whimsy?

In an age marred by burnout and algorithms, people are once again making room for the weird, the joyful, and the wonderfully unproductive. We tapped 18 tastemakers to learn how they’re doing it.

Let’s face it: Growing up is hard. One day, you’re listening to The Cheetah Girls soundtrack on the way to school with an armful of Silly Bandz and the next, you’re worried about making rent, hours lost to Google Sheets, and an unexpected medical bill when you still have a $52 request floating in a sea of Venmo transactions.

As for the world we’ve grown into? Nearly two years into Trump’s second presidential term, with AI raising questions about how much of our thinking we’ll hand over to machines, adult life can feel relentlessly optimized and not as chic or shiny as we imagined it as kids. But even if the future feels uncertain, we continue to hope. 2026 has revived the desire to make room for the colorful, playful, and wonderfully impractical again, and that impulse has a name: whimsy.

“As more of our everyday experiences are shaped by technology and algorithms, people are finding value in things that feel more personal, creative, and unexpected. Inspiration is still incredibly important, but so is the freedom to take what you see and make it your own,” Sydney Stanback, global trends and insights lead at Pinterest, tells Cosmopolitan.

“People are craving more imagination and fun in their everyday lives. There is a real appetite for aesthetics that feel less polished and predictable and more personal, and whimsy gives people room to try something they might not have considered before.”

And the numbers don’t lie. Pinterest searches for “bedazzling patterns” have increased by a whopping 9,187 percent this year, while “bedazzled phone case” searches are up 8,012 percent. Even “whimsical office outfits” have jumped 885 percent.

From Kylie Jenner’s bejeweled birthday cake and “princess kitty”–themed fete to the deconstructed silhouettes and glittery garments taking over spring/summer 2027 runways, we’re all finding increasingly creative ways to inject personality into our days. In an increasingly automated world, doing things simply because we can is the ultimate act of rebellion. At its core, whimsy is about making magic out of the mundane, whether we’re revisiting childhood obsessions now that we (sort of) have adult money or taking the DIY approach to life to make it more vibrant.

That’s why Cosmopolitan invited 18 colorful, charismatic, and unapologetically authentic tastemakers to share how they’re romanticizing life and embracing their eccentricities. Because to be whimsical is to be human!

#6945. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

#6944. These Are the Home Trends Gaining Ground for 2027, According to Zillow

A new analysis of millions of for-sale listings reveals the features sellers are increasingly highlighting.

Above: A color-drenched den on Long Island, New York, designed by General Assembly.

Homes are getting smarter, but sellers are also advertising places to unplug. Zillow’s 2027 Home Trends Report finds growing mentions of both AI and tech-free spaces, along with color-drenched rooms, home offices, and “catios.”

The report tracks year-over-year changes in mentions of hundreds of features across millions of for-sale listings. The results reflect how sellers and agents are marketing homes—not a survey of buyers or evidence that a particular feature will help a property sell. Still, they offer a look at the amenities and design details increasingly making their way into listing descriptions. Here are the main takeaways.

Mentions of AI are up 119 percent, the largest increase in this year’s report. Zillow points to AI-powered appliances among the features sellers are advertising, while mentions of app-controlled home systems rose 34 percent.

Spaces to unplug are also appearing 34 percent more often. The report describes an interest in both technology that simplifies daily tasks and designated places to take a break from it.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert, in a statement. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Sellers and their real estate agents are now highlighting spaces to unplug.”

Color-drenched spaces—rooms where the same color extends across the walls, ceiling, and trim—are up 84 percent in listing mentions, making them one of the report’s biggest increases. Zillow sees the approach as part of a move toward bolder, more polished interiors. Exterior finishes are gaining ground, too: painted brick is up 27 percent, and board-and-batten siding rose 24 percent.

Color drenching takes a shade beyond the walls and onto the ceiling, trim, and other architectural details. The approach has even spawned a bathroom variation: tile drenching, which extends the same tile across surfaces for a continuous look.

The language used to describe these homes is changing alongside the features. Mentions of “elevated” rose 71 percent, while “cozy” fell 22 percent, “eclectic” dropped 19 percent, and “boho” declined 13 percent. Cozy remains one of the most common words in listings, but it’s appearing less frequently than it did a year ago.

Kitchen language is moving in the other direction. Mentions of “chef’s kitchens” fell 21 percent, and “gourmet kitchens” declined 15 percent.

Mentions of multigenerational living jumped 58 percent. Zillow connects the increase to homes accommodating the “sandwich generation,” or people caring for children and older family members.

Features that can serve different ages and needs are also appearing more frequently. Grab bars are up 18 percent, wider doorways are up 13 percent, and nurseries rose 9 percent. The report places these changes alongside the growing emphasis on flexible rooms and spaces with more than one use.

The home spa is changing. Mentions of spa-inspired bathrooms rose 60 percent, and cold plunge pools increased 44 percent. Jacuzzis and jetted tubs, meanwhile, each fell about 9 percent.

Spaces for exercise are also gaining ground. Home gyms are up 18 percent, while golf simulators rose 29 percent. Together, the findings cover both workout spaces and amenities marketed around rest and recovery, rather than a single version of the home wellness room.

Cats get a category of their own this year. “Catios”—screened-in outdoor spaces that let indoor cats spend time outside—are appearing in 32 percent more listings. A place to get some air, watch the birds, and keep an eye on the neighbors—the cat has a wish list, too.

#6942. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

#6938. Jimmy Carter’s Family Farm Just Sold for $8.5 Million

The Georgia property will become part of a nearly 7,000-acre wildlife management area, with public access possible as early as 2027.

President Jimmy Carter’s family farmland near Plains, Georgia, was just sold to the Conservation Fund as part of an approximately $8.5 million acquisition that will protect nearly 2,500 acres of fields and woodland. Announced November 9, the deal sets the stage for the land’s eventual transfer to the state of Georgia, with public access potentially beginning as early as 2027.

The purchase encompasses approximately 1,600 acres owned by the Carter family, along with an adjoining tract. It does not include Carter’s boyhood home and farm, which are already preserved by the National Park Service as part of Jimmy Carter National Historical Park.

Instead, the newly acquired property is slated to join the nearby Lanahassee Creek Wildlife Management Area, expanding it to nearly 7,000 acres. The land provides habitat for gopher tortoises and other wildlife, while neighboring creeks support rare freshwater mussels. Plans call for the Georgia Department of Natural Resources to take ownership in 2028.

The acquisition also continues restoration work Carter initiated himself. At his request, the family preserved a stand of mature longleaf pine and replanted much of the surrounding acreage with the same native species. Once used to grow crops, including peanuts, the property became a retreat where he and Rosalynn spent time away from public life. The family acquired the land around the turn of the 20th century, according to the Conservation Fund.

“They liked to go and walk the land,” Blake Sullivan, who managed Carter’s farm and timberland for more than four decades, told The Washington Post. “For both of them, it was a place of rest and renewal.”

Carter continued visiting into his final years, arranging to be driven through the property when walking became difficult. Amy Carter, the president’s daughter, told The Post, “I think his very favorite place was to be in the town and on the land where he grew up, and where he had those very deep roots.”

#6935. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

Microsoft is introducing new tools within its sovereign cloud offerings designed to give organizations tighter control over where their data resides, who can access it, and how critical systems operate, especially in light of AI becoming a bigger part of enterprise infrastructure.

CoSN, the professional association for K-12 education technology leaders, has released new guidance to help schools navigate the screen time debate and develop technology policies that protect students while supporting learning.

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM.

Technology directors touch nearly everything that happens in a school system. Here are three strategies these IT leaders can use to navigate their many duties without losing strategic focus, influence, or sustainability.

#6934. What Is the Royal “Grid” and Why is the Palace Apparently “Infuriated” Over Prince Harry Wanting to Join It?

Prince Harry has been back in the UK for some weeks now, and he’s wasted no time in letting everyone know about it, with plenty of public moments including a surprise appearance on This Morning and a family outing to London's Winter Wonderland. But while fans of the Duke of Sussex celebrate his return, the Palace is said to be “infuriated” with his recent engagements and, in particular, his request to join the “grid.”

Speaking to The Express, royal commentator Richard Fitzwilliams accused Harry and Meghan Markle of purposely timing their public appointments to take attention away from other members of the royal family.

“We must assume this has been deliberate. This is deplorable,” the royal expert said, adding, “[The Palace is] indeed right to be infuriated.”

But while Fitzwilliams accuses Harry of intentionally scheduling his engagements to outshine his royal counterparts, perhaps it's all just bad timing—which is where the royal “grid” comes into things...

The royal “grid” is a meticulously organized schedule of the royal family’s engagements and appointments. Although the actual “grid” is not available to the public, fans of the royal family can keep up with their plans via the official website. According to the site, official royal engagements are published up to eight weeks in advance.

According to The Express, Prince Harry has requested that he be added to the royal “grid.” His motive for the move hasn’t been confirmed, but there are of course plenty of theories. Some critics, like royal commentator Fitzwilliams, believe that Harry wants to intentionally create more scheduling conflicts with the other royals. Others take the more agreeable stance that the duke simply wants to avoid any further calendar clashes.

One insider, for example, said that Harry is “mindful” of his fellow royals and their plans, and tries his best to avoid overlapping with them. “He knows about those things and he is obviously mindful of that as he’s planning his own activities as well,” the source told The Times.

The royal family is split into various households, with Clarence House taking care of King Charles and Queen Camilla and Kensington Palace looking after the Wales family, for example. These households are made up of large teams, covering everything from catering to housekeeping. Also in the royal household are those who plan and manage the family members’ calendars—these are overseen by private secretaries and diary coordinators.

A previous advertisement for such a job at Kensington Palace said, “The role will be responsible for managing the seamless delivery of multiple complex diaries to ensure the smooth and effective coordination of engagements and events.”

As for what the role involved? “Compiling and managing coordinated diary updates and weekly overviews; providing regular briefings; communicating diary changes to all key stakeholders, both internally and externally; and carrying out follow-up actions as necessary, as well as ensuring that all events and engagements are accurately recorded,” the listing explained.

“The successful candidate will have a proven ability to manage complex diaries and will have excellent communication skills,” the vacancy ad went on. Being a team player and having a proactive mindset were just some of the other requirements of the role, in addition to maintaining confidentiality and discretion, which the listing described as “paramount.”

#6931. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

#6929. This New Jaeger-LeCoultre Watch Looks Perfect for Robert Pattinson’s James Bond

The actor’s favorite watchmaker has unveiled a pink-gold Reverso with impeccable timing.

Just a week after Robert Pattinson was announced as the next James Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre, the Swiss watchmaker for which he's an ambassador, has unveiled a new Reverso that looks rather well suited to 007.

It's certainly a good match for Pattinson's personal tastes. “I definitely like really thin watches,” he said in a recent interview. “That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap.”

The new Reverso Classic Monoface Small Seconds checks both boxes. Measuring just 7.56mm thick, it has an 18-carat pink-gold case, a silver-colored dial with a texture inspired by stacked coins, and a black alligator strap. Its smaller proportions–40.1mm by 24.4mm—recall the original Reverso of 1931, whose ingenious swiveling case was designed to protect the watch during polo matches. Inside is a manually wound movement with a 42-hour power reserve.

At $18,400, it's a somewhat more elegant alternative to the more beefy dive watches traditionally associated with Bond. And with its art deco styling and slim profile, it would slip neatly beneath a dinner-jacket cuff with an ease the next Madeleine Swann would surely appreciate.

Of course, Omega has been Bond's official watch partner since 1995, and there's no suggestion that's changing. But as a watch for the man playing Bond, this new Reverso feels intriguingly on point.

#6927. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

#6926. Inside Danielle Lauder’s Barbie Dream Closet

Designed by AMMOR Architecture, the ultra-pink, mega dressing room is a cinematic fantasy come to life.

Cher Horowitz had a touch-screen conveyer belt in her color-coded closet. Carrie Bradshaw kept her Manolos in an unusually spacious high-fashion-filled walk-in. And Margot Robbie’s Barbie displayed her hot-pink wardrobe like doll’s packaging in her wall-free fantasy villa. Danielle Lauder, the great-granddaughter of Estée Lauder, created her own iteration of the dream closet—this time, in real life.

Her own inspiration? “I always thought of Belle in Beauty and the Beast in the library, just rolling around on her ladder,” she says. “I’m not much of a reader, but as a collector, I knew that’s what I wanted.”

Her wish list for the jewel-box space also included curving walls, glossy pink lacquer, illuminated marble, and a capsule-shaped vanity inspired by the makeup collection she designed for Estée Lauder in 2019. The beauty empire was founded by—and named after—her great-grandmother in 1946; and her father, William P. Lauder, serves as the chairman of the board. (Danielle now oversees her own production company, Act 4 Artists.)

To make her dream a reality, Lauder turned to architects Goil Amornvivat and Thomas Morbitzer, founders of New York City–based AMMOR Architecture. The duo had instant chemistry with Lauder, who was especially taken with the meticulous diorama they created for her.

“Tom and I both love Barbie,” says Amornvivat. “So we tweaked our design process to make an architectural model that was more like a dollhouse, where you could switch out surfaces.” Their efforts worked on Lauder, herself a Barbie fan, and the pair walked away with the commission to not just create the dressing room but to overhaul the entire industrial-style apartment in the West Village of Manhattan, which was then being used as an art studio.

“They both really understood and saw my vision,” says Lauder. “Plus, they were the most excited, which made me more excited. Goil is like the Energizer Bunny.”

The 250-square-foot dressing room is, Lauder says, “the biggest room in the apartment besides the living room.” It’s also where Lauder spends the most time. She enters just off the primary bedroom via an arched passageway finished in custom bronze plaster, emerging from the dark portal into a pink fantasia where her expansive fashion collection is arrayed like a personal boutique. “The idea was for it to be part display area but also a functional closet,” she says.

At the center is a custom pink vanity outfitted with fluted drawers, dual-sided mirrors, and a top made of richly veined Midnight Rose marble, sourced from New York Stone. Lauder keeps her collection of vintage and reissued Estée Lauder compacts in the drawers. “I love collecting them and having them out on display or in a nice drawer,” says Lauder. “They’re beautiful little artifacts.”

On the curving walls, rounded niches display shelving and racks against illuminated stone, part of Lauder’s request for layered lighting. “I love the backlit marble,” she says. “It’s so flattering.” She keeps her enviable bag and shoe collection on permanent display, maintained with help from her housekeeper. Lauder draws from the selection on a daily basis, with particular affection for family heirlooms such as her mom’s vintage Dolce & Gabbana handbags.

Overhead, Amornvivat and Morbitzer devised a futuristic lighting installation, stretching mesh taut over an oval-shaped light source to create a seamless surface. “The light in there is controllable by color, and it's dimmable,” notes Morbitzer. “It’s kind of like a skylight, which was important because it’s the only room without windows,” adds Amornvivat.

The bespoke elements continue: heated floors, an ice maker, a perfume bar, plenty of drawers, and a pair of walk-in closets cleverly concealed behind matching lacquered doors. “The whole room is custom millwork,” says Amornvivat.

And Lauder did get the sliding ladder of her dreams. “That was my hill to die on,” she says. “I’ve always struggled to reach things in my closet, so it was definitely practical. And my grandfather had one in his apartment in the library when I was growing up, and I just loved it.”

Now that the closet is complete, Lauder has a hard time leaving. “I spend more time in here than I do probably even sleeping in bed,” she says with a laugh. The space is remarkably faithful to the original idea that Amornvivat and Morbitzer presented in their dollhouse diorama. “I wanted it to be totally mine, feminine and fun, and still very warm and inviting,” she says. “It’s become my ultimate bachelorette hub.”

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6924. Front Office: Radhi Devlukia

The host of the A Really Good Cry podcast turned to designer Jess Cooney to create a home office-meets-studio made for deep conversations.

Radhi Devlukia isn’t afraid of tears. On her podcast, A Really Good Cry, the whole point is to get personal, and to do so with guests as varied as supermodel Miranda Kerr, dermatologists, hospice nurses, personal finance advisors—and Devlukia’s husband, fellow podcaster Jay Shetty.

Now, two years into the show, Devlukia, a best-selling author, cook, and founder of beverage company Juni, is unveiling a new studio and office space located within her and Shetty’s Los Angeles house. Devlukia turned to designer Jess Cooney, who has a namesake Berkshires-based interiors firm, to craft a space designed to support these really good cries. Cooney had never tackled a podcast studio before but was ready for the challenge, which included building a room that could both serve as a set—technical components, artificial light, and all—as well as a welcoming home office.

From the start, Cooney knew what the space would not include. “We wanted to move away from the tropes usually seen in podcast studios, like styled bookshelves or cluttered backgrounds that detract from the people being interviewed, and also anything logo or merch heavy,” she says. “And toward something that felt warmer and more inviting.”

Instead, she took cues from spaces like fashion designer Bella Freud’s London apartment, Jean Cocteau’s Villa Santo Sospir in the Côte d’Azur, and Paloma Elsesser’s home by Gregory Rockwell. The resulting office, says Cooney, “has this incredible womb-like, feminine atmosphere that cocoons and holds you.”

Below, Devlukia takes our Front Office survey, giving a behind-the-scenes look at her new home base.

Radhi Devlukia: The hanging art piece in the middle of the window, Kissed by Katie Burdon.

When you need to lock in and power through work, what’s your setup?

Doors open, facing the window, instrumental music playing. I also move areas a lot during work hours to stay focused, so sometimes I’m at the desk, sometimes on the swivel chairs, sometimes on the floor by the window!

I have articles printed about my grandma and grandad on my canvas memory wall. They taught at schools in Africa in the 1970s and there were articles written about how much they were doing for the community. I also have vintage ceramics—I love the mix of old and new.

It really depends. If there are two of us, we’re on the swivel chairs. If there are a few of us, we’re around the marble table. We love keeping things casual at the office so sometimes people sit on the cozy rug or wherever feels comfortable.

What was the best meeting you’ve had there?

We have had so many creative meetings in this office. It’s such a calming and flowing environment. The whole rebrand of the podcast was ideated in this new office, which I’m obsessed with now.

What would you change about it if you could?

Maybe just having the ability to shoot with the blinds open.

What’s the funniest thing that happened there?

My team loves to prank me, so there’s been a lot of laughter in our office!

Annie Goldsmith is the senior editor and digital lead at ELLE Decor, where she covers design, culture, style, and trends. She previously held positions at The Information, covering technology and culture, and Town & Country, writing about news, entertainment, and fashion. Her work has also appeared in Vogue, Rolling Stone, and the SF Standard.

#6923. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6918. Is This the Ultimate Aspen Anti-Chalet?

Designer Rafael de Cárdenas, known for creating arresting spaces for Skims, Nike, and a host of New York City restaurants, worked with longtime client Rachel Dorrell on a mountain retreat with otherworldly glam.

Above: The primary bathroom of a home in Aspen, Colorado, designed by Rafael de Cárdenas with architecture by SAOTA. Custom tub with Dornbracht fittings; ceramic vase by artist Kazunori Hamana; side table by Rafael Prieto.

“We’re having a crisis of taste,” architect and designer Rafael de Cárdenas is in the midst of saying. “There’s a sameness. It’s all a historical mishmash.” He’s at breakfast in Manhattan drinking a cinnamon latte (the restaurant did not have the turmeric he requested). He has already completed his run in Central Park and his gratitude list in his Day One journal app, and he’s on day 735 following a visit to the Hoffman Institute—the famously mysterious weeklong “personal growth” retreat. “I feel like we’re in The Handmaid’s Tale,” he says. “AI is happening, but we’re going to pretend we’re designing manor houses in the English countryside in the 19th century?” Later he adds, “I have never been interested in recreating the past.”

So when longtime client Rachel Dorrell asked him to create a mountain house in Aspen for her growing family, no one expected a typical ski chalet. From her first visit to the plot of land, she realized the views of the town and the slopes beyond would be unmatched. Today those “crazy views” are framed by windows so large it’s hard to tell where rooms end and the outside begins. De Cárdenas, who collaborated with SAOTA architects to create the modernist structure, set about realizing Dorrell’s vision of “Colorado, but refined,” as she puts it. “Glamorous ’80s apartment” is how he describes the results.

“The mood board was Angelo Donghia,” that great of American design who worked with the likes of Liza Minnelli. “And Beetlejuice, always Beetlejuice! And Mickey Rourke’s apartment in 91/2 Weeks.” A cheetah-print rug sits in the library, while sculpted white custom sofas anchor the immense yet minimalist living room. “So many people have asked, ‘Shouldn’t there be art above the fireplace?’ ” de Cárdenas says. “I’m like, ‘Why?’ ”

Following the rules has never been his style. It has been 20 years since he launched his design firm, after studying at UCLA and assisting architect Greg Lynn at Vienna’s Angewandte institute and MAK museum, then returning to his native New York. The son of an erstwhile fashion PR executive and an actuary, de Cárdenas aspired to work for David Rockwell or for Alex de Betak’s fashion production company, where he once applied for a job. In the end he did a stint at Calvin Klein and worked as a production designer for films and commercials. Then a friend asked for help redoing a kitchen, something he did “kind of at night,” de Cárdenas remembers. Next came his first real client: actress Parker Posey. It was a revelation. “She was the first person I ever had to make window treatments for. I was like, ‘What the f***?’ I remember looking at a window and being like, ‘What is this supposed to look like?’ I didn’t understand that residential interior design is, essentially, fabric.”

Around the same time, he landed his first major corporate client, Nike, and did the design of aNYthing, a Lower East Side boutique that became a nexus for skaters, artists, and musicians in the late 2000s. “No one was doing stores like that at the time,” says creative director Jen Brill, who worked with de Cárdenas on several Nike projects. “There was nothing minimal about it. It was loud, it was fun. It was the first store I saw that was like retail theater.”

Another friend, Felix Burrichter, a curator and the founder of Pin-Up architecture magazine, recalls de Cárdenas’s early fascination with the Memphis Group architects (“when it was still considered bad taste,” Burrichter says) as well as his willingness to experiment with materials, such as applying duct tape patterns to the walls of a Jeffrey Deitch exhibition in Rome, or wrapping an Ikea chair with fluorescent gaffer tape. “For the aNYthing store, I physically built a lot of those things, because there was no one to do it and no budget,” de Cárdenas says. He sometimes laments not being so hands-on anymore, now that he has a team of 18 based in New York and Paris. “There was a lot of energy in those projects.”

“His work is like Limelight–meets–Park Avenue,” says Burrichter, who calls de Cárdenas the heir to ELLE Decor A-List designer Peter Marino, a maestro who works with significant private clients alongside luxury behemoths such as Dior and Chanel. De Cárdenas may have moved from duct tape to gold leaf applied by European artisans, “but there is always that tension in Raf’s work,” Burrichter says, “the binary between decorating and being an architect, between uptown and the club.”

Dorrell, for her part, became aware of de Cárdenas a decade ago, when she and her husband purchased a home in the West Village that he had staged. “We were like, ‘Who did the interiors and who did the art? It’s perfect.’ ” She says they were drawn to the ’70s vibe and the peachy hues, an unusual shade at the time. “We didn’t change a thing.” Since then they have worked together on homes in Austin, New York, and currently their largest yet, in Palm Beach. Dorrell sends “rapid-fire” texts of inspiration ranging from tiled walls at Lake Como’s Villa d’Este to barstools in Paris. “Raf cracks me up—we are brutally honest with each other,” she says, explaining that her taste skews more traditional, while his is often edgier.

“Some of my projects have moments that are ugly, and they excite me,” he says. “Because something truly excellent, something that is pushing boundaries, is not going to land pleasurably at first.” He points to Madonna, a frequent touchstone and source of inspiration for him. “The first time you saw Blond Ambition, the first time you saw Madonna with a high ponytail and with the headset, it was a little jarring. The thick eyebrow and the cone bra.” He often finds himself thinking, Where is that moment now?

Despite his concerns about the flattening effect of the algorithm, he’s still happy he’s in the business of taste. “I can’t believe I was lucky enough to land here, because I didn’t know what interior design even was,” he says. “I like my job. I’m grateful for my job. I want people to say, ‘He’s good at his job.’ ”

The Dorrells might concur: “ ‘We’re not doing another project,’ ” Rachel recalls her husband saying about Aspen. “Now he’s like, ‘Thank god,’ because it’s so awesome.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

#6917. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

As threat actors adopt more targeted and convincing tactics, education leaders need practical ways to reduce risk without undermining the open, connected environments their institutions depend on.

#6914. The One Kitchen Feature Designers Always Splurge on—and Why It’s Worth It

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: Meghan Jay selected a brass chandelier with ribbed glass orbs to modernize this traditional kitchen.

The kitchen is the hardest-working room in your house, but it’s also one of the most expensive spaces to renovate. Depending on the size and scope of your redo, reimagining this space can cost up to six figures, so it’s important to be wise about allocating your budget. While some people may overspend on high-end appliances or really luxurious countertops, you should never overlook the room’s lighting. Turns out, that’s where designers always splurge in the kitchen.

“Lighting has such a powerful ability to change how a room feels,” explains Meghan Jay, an interior designer in Winnetka, Illinois. “You can have the most beautiful cabinetry, stone, and hardware, but if the lighting isn't right, the space can feel flat or even cold.”

Meghan Meehan of Hudson East Design in New Jersey agrees and says the right fixtures can provide height, dimensionality, and lots of mood to the space. “Don't think of lighting as the finishing touch,” she adds. “Think of it as one of the main design elements that makes the entire kitchen.”

Ahead, here’s why spending a little more on your kitchen lighting is worth it—and exactly how much you should budget for.

Every kitchen deserves to glow at full wattage, but it will cost you. After all, illuminating a kitchen isn’t as simple as adding a budget-friendly portable lamp and calling it a day: The best and brightest kitchens usually include several sources such as sconces, recessed ceiling fixtures, and under-cabinet lighting to illuminate meal-prepping zones.

“It needs to provide the right illumination for cooking and everyday tasks, but it also creates warmth and ambiance when you're entertaining or gathering with family,” Meehan explains. As a rule of thumb, she recommends homeowners allocate at least 8 to 12 percent of their kitchen renovation budget to lighting.

That said, anyone who wants to wow with some statement fixtures might wind up spending more. Jay, for example, suggests setting aside $3,000 to $7,000 for decorative wares such as pendant lights above an island.

Admittedly, spending thousands of dollars on lighting is bound to elicit some sticker shock, and some people may wonder if it’s worth it. However, Lauren Wodicka insists it’s worth the investment. “Unlike a sofa, rug, or an appliance, lighting doesn’t experience the same daily wear and tear,” the Northern Virginia designer reasons. “A beautiful, well-made fixture can last for generations and become the focal point of a room.”

Oftentimes, less expensive fixtures can read cheap. “You may notice overly shiny or artificial-looking brass finishes, visible seams, thin metal, or cheaper-looking glass,” Jay says. “Higher-quality fixtures often have more substantial materials, beautiful patinas, and thoughtful details that become more apparent the closer you are.”

If you want to get the best bang for your buck, devise a shopping strategy. Santa Monica-based designer Christine Markatos Lowe first selects functional lighting and then spends the rest of her budget on “decorative pieces with personality.” But make no mistake, a personable light fixture should still have staying power: Lowe often reaches for timeless silhouettes from retailers like Urban Electric and Ann-Morris Lighting and is a sucker for details like handblown glass or “beautifully finished metals.”

“The key to finding the right balance between statement lighting and a cohesive kitchen design is to make sure every element feels intentional,” Lowe explains. “It’s all about allowing each material or element to have its moment without overwhelming the space.”Wodicka—who calls Soane, Hudson Valley Lighting, and Visual Comfort her go-tos—says less is more. “Not every kitchen island needs two or three lighting pendants,” she explains. “Sometimes one beautiful fixture can make a stronger statement.”

Designers often say lighting is the jewelry of a room, so layering different materials, sizes, and finishes adds more visual intrigue. Wodicka wants kitchen lights to complement each other—and the fixtures in surrounding rooms—but they don’t have to be identical. “A light fixture doesn't necessarily have to match the cabinet hardware or plumbing fixtures,” Jay adds. “I often prefer mixing finishes to create a more collected, layered look.”

If you want to keep your fixtures cohesive—without identical finishes—stick with a particular style. For example, a sconce with pleated fabric shades will look out of place next to a midcentury-style pendant that looks like it belongs on the set of Mad Men.

For the perfect finishing touch, Meehan recommends putting all lights on dimmers, which is a simple detail that can make a huge difference. “This allows for functional lighting when cooking, but softer light to warm the space when you are entertaining,” she adds. And, if a small detail can make your investment work even harder, even better.

#6913. Jackie Kennedy’s Private Letters to Truman Capote Reveal a Fuller Picture of Their Friendship

Heading to auction on November 21, the six notes are estimated to fetch as much as $80,000.

First Lady Jacqueline Kennedy’s sister, Lee Radziwill, was one of celebrated author Truman Capote’s “Swans,” developing a close, if complicated, friendship with the novelist. But Jackie had her own relationship with Capote, one that is less widely known. The pair corresponded with each other in the 1960s and Kennedy may have even gotten an early look at Capote’s 1966 novel, In Cold Blood.

“I have your precious book and am so overjoyed,” she wrote to Capote in an undated card, presumed to be from June 1963. “The minute Jack takes off for Europe I will retreat to the country + indulge myself by reading every page—not just the new ones.”

That letter, along with five others between Kennedy and Capote, will soon hit the auction block, ELLE Decor can reveal. The notes, three of which are written on White House letterhead, will be sold together in a single lot through Cornell Auctions, estimated to fetch between $40,000 and $80,000. Together, they reveal new details about the friendship between the former first lady and one of America’s great authors.

Available for the first time ever, these private letters are part of a larger auction of Capote-linked items accumulated over decades by his biographer, Gerald Clarke, author of 1988’s Capote. The sale, “The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote,” will take place on November 21.

Kennedy’s notes are all believed to have been written in 1963 and 1964, with four bearing postmark dates. During that period, Kennedy suffered personal tragedies, losing her infant son Patrick days after he was born as well as her husband, President John F. Kennedy, who was assassinated in November 1963.

The letters indicate a warm relationship between Kennedy and Capote. In several of the messages, the first lady makes reference to various presents and mementos she received from the writer.

One particularly meaningful gift came in the wake of President John F. Kennedy’s death, after Jackie and Capote saw each other at a gathering hosted by decorator Billy Baldwin. “The most beautiful porcelain rose that I ever saw or dreamed of—came to me the other day from you,” the former first lady wrote in a three-page December 1964 missive, handwritten on mourning stationery bearing a crest.

In another letter, Kennedy makes reference to a gift she sent to Capote, inspired by a visit to the novelist’s home. “In your Japanese-Victorian room I spotted two large apothecary jars of the same kind—I found this one in 1955 in Paris—so it has been with me for many years in many places,” she wrote in an undated letter, believed to be from December 1964.

Handwritten on black-bordered stationery, that note was addressed to Capote’s house at 70 Willow Street in Brooklyn Heights, where he wrote Breakfast at Tiffany’s and In Cold Blood.

Read Jackie Kennedy’s Letters to Truman Capote

Beyond the Kennedy notes, also up for auction is Capote’s four-piece formal dinner invitation from President Lyndon B. Johnson in 1966, which has an estimate of $600 to $2,000. Other highlights include a German pencil sharpener in a leather pouch; a chalkware bulldog; a monogrammed duffel bag; and jewel-like paperweights made by the author himself.

Taken together, these pieces paint a fuller picture of Capote’s life and work as a writer, as well as Kennedy’s enthusiastic support of his talents. “I keep thinking what power a great writer has. All the things you write move people,” she wrote. “I am glad you became a writer.”

“The Magpie and the Swans: The Possessions of Truman Capote” will take place November 21 starting at 11 a.m.; bidding can be done online via LiveAuctioneers or in person at Cornell Auctions in Bellport, New York.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6910. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

#6906. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

Organizations are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

As threat actors adopt more targeted and convincing tactics, education leaders need practical ways to reduce risk without undermining the open, connected environments their institutions depend on.
